Like I’ll be sitting there after dinner thinking, okay, I need to send that follow-up or confirm a lunch, and then I start turning it over too much and it doesn’t get done.\n\nIt’s funny, I used to be pretty good at keeping all those little threads going. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Completely wrong? I mean… I guess it’s possible. I can say that, sitting here. Like, on paper, sure — invitations go out to lots of people, panels get planned months ahead, seating charts are probably some event planner trying to balance donors and board members. My father used to always say Cincinnati feels like a big neighborhood pretending to be a city, so overlaps happen.

But then when I’m actually in it, it doesn’t feel random. I came in because for a while now I’ve been pretty convinced certain people in the arts and finance circles here are quietly tracking which clients I meet with and sending me little signals — through invitations, panel topics, even who ends up sitting near me at events.

So… possible I’m completely wrong, yes. But I don’t feel completely wrong. Even when I tell myself it could be coincidence, I can’t really let it go.

I’m still working, I mean, I still have clients and I can do the actual advising part fine when I’m sitting with them. But it’s… it’s gotten harder to keep the practice at the same size.\n\nLike, I’ll miss follow-ups because I’m second-guessing whether reaching out to someone is going to put me back into one of those circles, or I’ll avoid going to a public panel I probably should attend. And those panels are where you run into people, you know — you see the same faces from the museum board or someone’s charity thing, and then somehow that connects back to a client lunch, and it just gets messy in my head.\n\nSo with colleagues, I guess yes, because I’m not as present. I don’t keep up the same level of client contact I used to. My practice has gotten smaller, not collapsed or anything, but smaller. Friends and family… less directly, maybe, but work is the main place it shows.\n\nDoctor: Are there activities outside of work that you've stopped doing or avoided because of this feeling of being tracked?\n\nPatient: Yeah… outside of work too, I guess. Not in some dramatic way, like I’m locked in my apartment or anything. I still do things, sort of, in pieces.\n\nBut I’ve skipped jazz nights I used to go to, and pickup basketball, things like that. Sometimes it’s because I don’t want to be around the same overlapping crowd, but sometimes it’s just… I don’t have the spark for it. Like I’ll think about going, and then I just don’t, and I don’t even feel that bothered.\n\nFriends will text and ask what’s going on, and I’ll give these short answers. Not because I’m mad at them. Talking just feels like more effort than it should. Even with work, I still get things done, but follow-ups sit there longer than they should. It’s all kind of slowed down.\n\nDoctor: Are there any everyday tasks or routines that you've had difficulty with because of these feelings of being tracked?\n\nPatient: Yeah, the routine stuff is mostly work routines, I guess. Like follow-ups. I’ll mean to call someone back or send the email after a meeting, and then I start thinking about whether that contact pulls me back into one of those circles, and I put it off.\n\nIt sounds small, but that’s kind of the whole practice, you know — keeping contact, staying visible, going to panels where people remember you. I used to be better about that. Now I skip some of those public things, and then I’m not seeing clients or colleagues as much, and the client contact drops off.\n\nSo I’m still working. I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t function. But my practice has definitely gotten smaller because I’m not keeping up with those routines the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these signals or the tracking are meant to harm you or benefit you in some way?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s exactly to help me. Harm… I don’t know, maybe not physically or anything. It feels more like they’re trying to keep tabs on me, steer me a little, or see who I’m meeting with.\n\nLike someone will invite me to an arts fundraiser, and then the panel topic is weirdly close to something I talked about with a client, or someone from the finance side sits near me, and I start thinking, okay, that’s not random. And Cincinnati is small in that way — you see the same people at museum things, charity boards, client lunches — so it all overlaps.\n\nI tell myself it could just be coincidence, but I can’t really let it go. It feels like signals, like they’re watching which clients I’m connected to. Not necessarily “we’re going to hurt you,” but definitely not innocent either.\n\nDoctor: Are there any parts of your daily life, outside of work and social events, where you feel like these beliefs are affecting your behavior or decisions?\n\nPatient: Outside of work and social stuff… I mean, it mostly shows up through the work routines, even when I’m at home. Like I’ll be sitting there after dinner thinking, okay, I need to send that follow-up or confirm a lunch, and then I start turning it over too much and it doesn’t get done.\n\nIt’s funny, I used to be pretty good at keeping all those little threads going. My calendar looked ridiculous, honestly — panels, coffees, client check-ins, that kind of thing. My dad used to say half the job was just showing up where people could see you, which annoyed me, but he wasn’t wrong.\n\nNow I’m still working, I don’t want to make it sound like everything stopped. But the practice has gotten smaller. I miss follow-ups, I avoid some public panels, and I’m not keeping up the same level of client contact I used to. So I guess, yeah, it affects decisions day to day, just in these boring little ways that add up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people would agree that these signals and tracking are real?\n\nPatient: I think… most people would probably say I’m reading too much into it, yeah. Especially if they’re not in those circles. They’d say Cincinnati is just small, everyone knows everyone, panel topics repeat, seating is random — that kind of thing.\n\nBut when you’re actually there, and you see the same arts people and finance people crossing over, and then an invitation shows up right after I meet a client, or the topic sounds a little too close to something I was just dealing with… it’s hard for me to just shrug that off.\n\nSo, would other people agree? Maybe not out loud. Some might. I don’t know. I just know I came in because I’ve been pretty convinced for a while that certain people are quietly tracking who I meet with and sending these little signals through invitations, panels, even who sits near me. I can tell myself it might be coincidence, but I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that the signals or tracking you believe are happening are specifically about you, or could they be about someone else?\n\nPatient: I think it’s about me, mostly. Or at least it feels centered on me — who I’m meeting, which clients I’m connected to, where I show up.\n\nI mean, maybe other people are part of it too, because those circles overlap so much here. Cincinnati finance and arts boards, fundraisers, museum events… it’s always the same handful of people, and everybody sort of knows somebody’s business. My father used to say that was just networking, and maybe that’s true, but it can feel different when you’re the one walking into the room and suddenly the panel topic sounds like something from a client conversation.\n\nSo… I don’t think it’s just some general thing happening to everyone. I came in because I’ve been pretty convinced that certain people in those arts and finance circles are quietly tracking which clients I meet with and sending little signals to me — invitations, topics, even who sits near me. I can tell myself it could be coincidence, but I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered the possibility that your interpretations of these events might be incorrect?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. I mean, I wouldn’t be here if some part of me didn’t think, okay, maybe I’m connecting dots that aren’t actually connected.\n\nBut then it’s like… I’ll tell myself it could just be Cincinnati being Cincinnati, you know, the same arts board people and finance people showing up everywhere. My father used to joke that you can’t get coffee downtown without three people knowing about it by lunch, which is probably true. And then I’ll get an invitation, or a panel topic will line up a little too neatly with a client I just met, or someone gets seated near me at an event, and I’m right back to thinking, no, this is directed at me.\n\nSo yes, I’ve considered I might be wrong. I just can’t really let go of the feeling that certain people are quietly tracking who I meet with and sending signals through those things.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that you might be completely wrong about people tracking you and sending you signals?\n\nPatient: Completely wrong? I mean… I guess it’s possible. I can say that, sitting here. Like, on paper, sure — invitations go out to lots of people, panels get planned months ahead, seating charts are probably some event planner trying to balance donors and board members. My father used to always say Cincinnati feels like a big neighborhood pretending to be a city, so overlaps happen.\n\nBut then when I’m actually in it, it doesn’t feel random. I came in because for a while now I’ve been pretty convinced certain people in the arts and finance circles here are quietly tracking which clients I meet with and sending me little signals — through invitations, panel topics, even who ends up sitting near me at events.\n\nSo… possible I’m completely wrong, yes. But I don’t feel completely wrong. Even when I tell myself it could be coincidence, I can’t really let it go.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Cincinnati finance and arts boards, fundraisers, museum events… it’s always the same handful of people, and everybody sort of knows somebody’s business. My father used to say that was just networking, and maybe that’s true, but it can feel different when you’re the one walking into the room and suddenly the panel topic sounds like something from a client conversation.\n\nSo… I don’t think it’s just some general thing happening to everyone. I came in because I’ve been pretty convinced that certain people in those arts and finance circles are quietly tracking which clients I meet with and sending little signals to me — invitations, topics, even who sits near me. I can tell myself it could be coincidence, but I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered the possibility that your interpretations of these events might be incorrect?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. I mean, I wouldn’t be here if some part of me didn’t think, okay, maybe I’m connecting dots that aren’t actually connected.\n\nBut then it’s like… I’ll tell myself it could just be Cincinnati being Cincinnati, you know, the same arts board people and finance people showing up everywhere. My father used to joke that you can’t get coffee downtown without three people knowing about it by lunch, which is probably true. And then I’ll get an invitation, or a panel topic will line up a little too neatly with a client I just met, or someone gets seated near me at an event, and I’m right back to thinking, no, this is directed at me.\n\nSo yes, I’ve considered I might be wrong. I just can’t really let go of the feeling that certain people are quietly tracking who I meet with and sending signals through those things.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that you might be completely wrong about people tracking you and sending you signals?\n\nPatient: Completely wrong? I mean… I guess it’s possible. I can say that, sitting here. Like, on paper, sure — invitations go out to lots of people, panels get planned months ahead, seating charts are probably some event planner trying to balance donors and board members. My father used to always say Cincinnati feels like a big neighborhood pretending to be a city, so overlaps happen.\n\nBut then when I’m actually in it, it doesn’t feel random. I came in because for a while now I’ve been pretty convinced certain people in the arts and finance circles here are quietly tracking which clients I meet with and sending me little signals — through invitations, panel topics, even who ends up sitting near me at events.\n\nSo… possible I’m completely wrong, yes. But I don’t feel completely wrong. Even when I tell myself it could be coincidence, I can’t really let it go.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had a moment where you were completely certain about one of these signals, but later found out it was actually just a coincidence or misunderstanding?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 32 / personal_financial_advisor / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"I came in because for a while now I’ve been convinced that certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking which clients I meet with and sending me little signals through invitations, panel topics, even who sits near me at events, and even when I tell myself it could just be coincidence, I can’t really let it go.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m trying to think of a clean example like that… where I was sure and then someone proved it was nothing. I don’t know that I have one that neat.

There have been times I’ve told myself afterward, “Okay, maybe that was just the seating chart,” or “maybe that panel was planned before I even met that client.” But then I start thinking about who was in the room, who sent the invitation, how Cincinnati’s arts and finance people all overlap — I mean, those museum dinners and fundraiser committees, it’s not like strangers, it’s the same names circling around — and it starts feeling intentional again.

So… I can’t say I’ve found out for sure it was just a coincidence. That’s kind of the problem. Even when I try to explain it that way, I don’t really let it go.

---------- turn 15 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------
========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F23\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I came in because something’s been bothering me for a while. I work in finance, and I go to these arts events and panels around Cincinnati sometimes — networking, mostly, though sometimes the food is actually decent, depending on who’s sponsoring it — and I’ve gotten this strong sense that certain people in those circles are keeping track of which clients I meet with.\n\nIt’s not like anyone has said it outright. It’s more… the invitations I get, the topics on panels, who ends up sitting near me at events. It feels like little signals. And I try to tell myself, okay, maybe that’s just coincidence, people sit where they sit, committees pick topics months ahead — but I can’t really shake it. It stays with me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling like you're being tracked or sent signals at these events?\n\nPatient: I’d say… about two months now. Maybe a little more, but two months is when I really started noticing it and couldn’t brush it off.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the usual networking stuff — people talk, people know people, Cincinnati can feel weirdly small that way — but then it kept happening, like the seating or the timing of invitations after certain client meetings. So, yeah… around two months that it’s been sticking with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about being tracked or sent signals every day, or are there days when you don't think about it at all?\n\nPatient: Most days, yeah, it comes up. Not like I’m sitting there every minute thinking about it, but if I get an invitation or I see a panel topic, or even I remember who was near me at one of those events, it sort of starts again.\n\nThere are maybe days where work is busy and I’m focused on client stuff, but even then it’s in the background. Like I’ll tell myself, “Okay, maybe it’s coincidence,” because I know committees plan these things and Cincinnati is small — I mean, everybody seems to know somebody through the arts boards or finance circles — but I can’t really let it go. It keeps feeling like they’re quietly tracking who I meet with and sending little signals.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe these people are actually tracking you or sending you signals, or is it just a feeling you can't shake?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know how it sounds, so part of me wants to say it’s just a feeling. But honestly, it feels more real than that. I’ve been pretty convinced that certain people in those arts and finance circles here are keeping track of which clients I’m meeting with.\n\nIt’s things like the invitations coming at odd times, the panel topics lining up too closely, or someone ending up sitting near me after I’ve had a certain meeting. And Cincinnati’s small, you know, people overlap everywhere — boards, fundraisers, client networks — so I try to tell myself maybe it’s all coincidence. But I can’t really let it go. It feels like they’re sending signals, quietly.\n\nDoctor: How convinced are you that these people are actually tracking you or sending you signals?\n\nPatient: I’d say… pretty convinced. Not like I have a spreadsheet proving it or anything, but it’s more than just a weird feeling.\n\nIt’s the same circles, you know — arts boards, finance people, those events downtown — and then after I meet with certain clients, suddenly there’s an invitation that feels timed strangely, or a panel topic that’s a little too close, or someone ends up seated near me who shouldn’t really be part of that conversation. And Cincinnati’s funny that way, everybody overlaps, like you go to one fundraiser and then suddenly someone from a totally different firm knows your whole week somehow.\n\nSo I do try to tell myself, okay, maybe committees planned this months ago, maybe it’s coincidence. But I can’t really let it go. I came in because I’ve been convinced, at least most of the time, that they’re quietly tracking who I’m meeting with and sending little signals about it.\n\nDoctor: Has this feeling of being tracked affected your relationships with friends, family, or colleagues?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. I’m still working, I mean, I still have clients and I can do the actual advising part fine when I’m sitting with them. But it’s… it’s gotten harder to keep the practice at the same size.\n\nLike, I’ll miss follow-ups because I’m second-guessing whether reaching out to someone is going to put me back into one of those circles, or I’ll avoid going to a public panel I probably should attend. And those panels are where you run into people, you know — you see the same faces from the museum board or someone’s charity thing, and then somehow that connects back to a client lunch, and it just gets messy in my head.\n\nSo with colleagues, I guess yes, because I’m not as present. I don’t keep up the same level of client contact I used to. My practice has gotten smaller, not collapsed or anything, but smaller. Friends and family… less directly, maybe, but work is the main place it shows.\n\nDoctor: Are there activities outside of work that you've stopped doing or avoided because of this feeling of being tracked?\n\nPatient: Yeah… outside of work too, I guess. Not in some dramatic way, like I’m locked in my apartment or anything. I still do things, sort of, in pieces.\n\nBut I’ve skipped jazz nights I used to go to, and pickup basketball, things like that. Sometimes it’s because I don’t want to be around the same overlapping crowd, but sometimes it’s just… I don’t have the spark for it. Like I’ll think about going, and then I just don’t, and I don’t even feel that bothered.\n\nFriends will text and ask what’s going on, and I’ll give these short answers. Not because I’m mad at them. Talking just feels like more effort than it should. Even with work, I still get things done, but follow-ups sit there longer than they should. It’s all kind of slowed down.\n\nDoctor: Are there any everyday tasks or routines that you've had difficulty with because of these feelings of being tracked?\n\nPatient: Yeah, the routine stuff is mostly work routines, I guess. Like follow-ups. I’ll mean to call someone back or send the email after a meeting, and then I start thinking about whether that contact pulls me back into one of those circles, and I put it off.\n\nIt sounds small, but that’s kind of the whole practice, you know — keeping contact, staying visible, going to panels where people remember you. I used to be better about that. Now I skip some of those public things, and then I’m not seeing clients or colleagues as much, and the client contact drops off.\n\nSo I’m still working. I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t function. But my practice has definitely gotten smaller because I’m not keeping up with those routines the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these signals or the tracking are meant to harm you or benefit you in some way?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s exactly to help me. Harm… I don’t know, maybe not physically or anything. It feels more like they’re trying to keep tabs on me, steer me a little, or see who I’m meeting with.\n\nLike someone will invite me to an arts fundraiser, and then the panel topic is weirdly close to something I talked about with a client, or someone from the finance side sits near me, and I start thinking, okay, that’s not random. And Cincinnati is small in that way — you see the same people at museum things, charity boards, client lunches — so it all overlaps.\n\nI tell myself it could just be coincidence, but I can’t really let it go. It feels like signals, like they’re watching which clients I’m connected to. Not necessarily “we’re going to hurt you,” but definitely not innocent either.\n\nDoctor: Are there any parts of your daily life, outside of work and social events, where you feel like these beliefs are affecting your behavior or decisions?\n\nPatient: Outside of work and social stuff… I mean, it mostly shows up through the work routines, even when I’m at home. Like I’ll be sitting there after dinner thinking, okay, I need to send that follow-up or confirm a lunch, and then I start turning it over too much and it doesn’t get done.\n\nIt’s funny, I used to be pretty good at keeping all those little threads going. My calendar looked ridiculous, honestly — panels, coffees, client check-ins, that kind of thing. My dad used to say half the job was just showing up where people could see you, which annoyed me, but he wasn’t wrong.\n\nNow I’m still working, I don’t want to make it sound like everything stopped. But the practice has gotten smaller. I miss follow-ups, I avoid some public panels, and I’m not keeping up the same level of client contact I used to. So I guess, yeah, it affects decisions day to day, just in these boring little ways that add up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people would agree that these signals and tracking are real?\n\nPatient: I think… most people would probably say I’m reading too much into it, yeah. Especially if they’re not in those circles. They’d say Cincinnati is just small, everyone knows everyone, panel topics repeat, seating is random — that kind of thing.\n\nBut when you’re actually there, and you see the same arts people and finance people crossing over, and then an invitation shows up right after I meet a client, or the topic sounds a little too close to something I was just dealing with… it’s hard for me to just shrug that off.\n\nSo, would other people agree? Maybe not out loud. Some might. I don’t know. I just know I came in because I’ve been pretty convinced for a while that certain people are quietly tracking who I meet with and sending these little signals through invitations, panels, even who sits near me. I can tell myself it might be coincidence, but I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that the signals or tracking you believe are happening are specifically about you, or could they be about someone else?\n\nPatient: I think it’s about me, mostly. Or at least it feels centered on me — who I’m meeting, which clients I’m connected to, where I show up.\n\nI mean, maybe other people are part of it too, because those circles overlap so much here. Cincinnati finance and arts boards, fundraisers, museum events… it’s always the same handful of people, and everybody sort of knows somebody’s business. My father used to say that was just networking, and maybe that’s true, but it can feel different when you’re the one walking into the room and suddenly the panel topic sounds like something from a client conversation.\n\nSo… I don’t think it’s just some general thing happening to everyone. I came in because I’ve been pretty convinced that certain people in those arts and finance circles are quietly tracking which clients I meet with and sending little signals to me — invitations, topics, even who sits near me. I can tell myself it could be coincidence, but I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered the possibility that your interpretations of these events might be incorrect?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. I mean, I wouldn’t be here if some part of me didn’t think, okay, maybe I’m connecting dots that aren’t actually connected.\n\nBut then it’s like… I’ll tell myself it could just be Cincinnati being Cincinnati, you know, the same arts board people and finance people showing up everywhere. My father used to joke that you can’t get coffee downtown without three people knowing about it by lunch, which is probably true. And then I’ll get an invitation, or a panel topic will line up a little too neatly with a client I just met, or someone gets seated near me at an event, and I’m right back to thinking, no, this is directed at me.\n\nSo yes, I’ve considered I might be wrong. I just can’t really let go of the feeling that certain people are quietly tracking who I meet with and sending signals through those things.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that you might be completely wrong about people tracking you and sending you signals?\n\nPatient: Completely wrong? I mean… I guess it’s possible. I can say that, sitting here. My father used to always say Cincinnati feels like a big neighborhood pretending to be a city, so overlaps happen.\n\nBut then when I’m actually in it, it doesn’t feel random. I came in because for a while now I’ve been pretty convinced certain people in the arts and finance circles here are quietly tracking which clients I meet with and sending me little signals — through invitations, panel topics, even who ends up sitting near me at events.\n\nSo… possible I’m completely wrong, yes. But I don’t feel completely wrong. Even when I tell myself it could be coincidence, I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a moment where you were completely certain about one of these signals, but later found out it was actually just a coincidence or misunderstanding?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a clean example like that… where I was sure and then someone proved it was nothing. I don’t know that I have one that neat.\n\nThere have been times I’ve told myself afterward, “Okay, maybe that was just the seating chart,” or “maybe that panel was planned before I even met that client.” But then I start thinking about who was in the room, who sent the invitation, how Cincinnati’s arts and finance people all overlap — I mean, those museum dinners and fundraiser committees, it’s not like strangers, it’s the same names circling around — and it starts feeling intentional again.\n\nSo… I can’t say I’ve found out for sure it was just a coincidence. That’s kind of the problem. Even when I try to explain it that way, I don’t really let it go.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Cincinnati finance and arts boards, fundraisers, museum events… it’s always the same handful of people, and everybody sort of knows somebody’s business. My father used to say that was just networking, and maybe that’s true, but it can feel different when you’re the one walking into the room and suddenly the panel topic sounds like something from a client conversation.\n\nSo… I don’t think it’s just some general thing happening to everyone. I can tell myself it could be coincidence, but I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered the possibility that your interpretations of these events might be incorrect?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. My father used to joke that you can’t get coffee downtown without three people knowing about it by lunch, which is probably true. And then I’ll get an invitation, or a panel topic will line up a little too neatly with a client I just met, or someone gets seated near me at an event, and I’m right back to thinking, no, this is directed at me.\n\nSo yes, I’ve considered I might be wrong. I just can’t really let go of the feeling that certain people are quietly tracking who I meet with and sending signals through those things.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that you might be completely wrong about people tracking you and sending you signals?\n\nPatient: Completely wrong? My father used to always say Cincinnati feels like a big neighborhood pretending to be a city, so overlaps happen.\n\nBut then when I’m actually in it, it doesn’t feel random. I mean… evidence. Yeah, I’ve thought about that a little, but it gets slippery.

If someone could show me, I guess, like actual planning emails or something that proves the invitation list was set before I met a client, or that the panel topic had nothing to do with me… maybe that would help. And Cincinnati is small in that way — you see the same people at museum things, charity boards, client lunches — so it all overlaps.\n\nI tell myself it could just be coincidence, but I can’t really let it go. Like I’ll be sitting there after dinner thinking, okay, I need to send that follow-up or confirm a lunch, and then I start turning it over too much and it doesn’t get done.\n\nIt’s funny, I used to be pretty good at keeping all those little threads going. My calendar looked ridiculous, honestly — panels, coffees, client check-ins, that kind of thing. But the practice has gotten smaller. I miss follow-ups, I avoid some public panels, and I’m not keeping up the same level of client contact I used to. So I guess, yeah, it affects decisions day to day, just in these boring little ways that add up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people would agree that these signals and tracking are real?\n\nPatient: I think… most people would probably say I’m reading too much into it, yeah. Especially if they’re not in those circles. I just know I came in because I’ve been pretty convinced for a while that certain people are quietly tracking who I meet with and sending these little signals through invitations, panels, even who sits near me. I can tell myself it might be coincidence, but I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that the signals or tracking you believe are happening are specifically about you, or could they be about someone else?\n\nPatient: I think it’s about me, mostly. Cincinnati finance and arts boards, fundraisers, museum events… it’s always the same handful of people, and everybody sort of knows somebody’s business. I just can’t really let go of the feeling that certain people are quietly tracking who I meet with and sending signals through those things.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that you might be completely wrong about people tracking you and sending you signals?\n\nPatient: Completely wrong? I mean… I guess it’s possible. I can say that, sitting here. Like, on paper, sure — invitations go out to lots of people, panels get planned months ahead, seating charts are probably some event planner trying to balance donors and board members. My father used to always say Cincinnati feels like a big neighborhood pretending to be a city, so overlaps happen.\n\nBut then when I’m actually in it, it doesn’t feel random. I came in because for a while now I’ve been pretty convinced certain people in the arts and finance circles here are quietly tracking which clients I meet with and sending me little signals — through invitations, panel topics, even who ends up sitting near me at events.\n\nSo… possible I’m completely wrong, yes. But I don’t feel completely wrong. Even when I tell myself it could be coincidence, I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a moment where you were completely certain about one of these signals, but later found out it was actually just a coincidence or misunderstanding?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a clean example like that… where I was sure and then someone proved it was nothing. I don’t know that I have one that neat.\n\nThere have been times I’ve told myself afterward, “Okay, maybe that was just the seating chart,” or “maybe that panel was planned before I even met that client.” But then I start thinking about who was in the room, who sent the invitation, how Cincinnati’s arts and finance people all overlap — I mean, those museum dinners and fundraiser committees, it’s not like strangers, it’s the same names circling around — and it starts feeling intentional again.\n\nSo… I can’t say I’ve found out for sure it was just a coincidence. That’s kind of the problem. Even when I try to explain it that way, I don’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any evidence that would make you change your mind about people tracking you and sending you signals?\n\nPatient: I mean… evidence. Yeah, I’ve thought about that a little, but it gets slippery.\n\nIf someone could show me, I guess, like actual planning emails or something that proves the invitation list was set before I met a client, or that the panel topic had nothing to do with me… maybe that would help. Or if the seating was just automated or handled by someone who doesn’t know me at all.\n\nBut then I hear myself saying that and I think, well, people in Cincinnati know people. Especially in the arts and finance circles — it’s all donors, boards, clients, gallery people, the same handful of last names. It’s all kind of slowed down.\n\nDoctor: Are there any everyday tasks or routines that you've had difficulty with because of these feelings of being tracked?\n\nPatient: Yeah, the routine stuff is mostly work routines, I guess. Like follow-ups. I’ll mean to call someone back or send the email after a meeting, and then I start thinking about whether that contact pulls me back into one of those circles, and I put it off.\n\nIt sounds small, but that’s kind of the whole practice, you know — keeping contact, staying visible, going to panels where people remember you. I used to be better about that. Now I skip some of those public things, and then I’m not seeing clients or colleagues as much, and the client contact drops off.\n\nSo I’m still working. I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t function. But my practice has definitely gotten smaller because I’m not keeping up with those routines the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these signals or the tracking are meant to harm you or benefit you in some way?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s exactly to help me. Harm… I don’t know, maybe not physically or anything. And Cincinnati is small in that way — you see the same people at museum things, charity boards, client lunches — so it all overlaps.\n\nI tell myself it could just be coincidence, but I can’t really let it go. Like I’ll be sitting there after dinner thinking, okay, I need to send that follow-up or confirm a lunch, and then I start turning it over too much and it doesn’t get done.\n\nIt’s funny, I used to be pretty good at keeping all those little threads going. My calendar looked ridiculous, honestly — panels, coffees, client check-ins, that kind of thing. I just know I came in because I’ve been pretty convinced for a while that certain people are quietly tracking who I meet with and sending these little signals through invitations, panels, even who sits near me. I can tell myself it might be coincidence, but I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that the signals or tracking you believe are happening are specifically about you, or could they be about someone else?\n\nPatient: I think it’s about me, mostly. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’ve considered it, yes. I mean, I’m not trying to be impossible about it. There are times when I think, okay, maybe I’m stitching things together because Cincinnati is… you know, it’s small in that weird way, especially with arts boards and finance people and donors, everybody knowing everybody’s business.

But I came in because for a while now I’ve been convinced that certain people in those circles are quietly tracking which clients I meet with and then sending me signals — invitations that show up right after a meeting, panel topics that feel too close, certain people seated near me at events. And even when I tell myself, “Maybe if I had more information, maybe it’s just coincidence,” I can’t really let it go. It still feels like there’s something behind it.

---------- turn 17 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------
========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F23\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I came in because something’s been bothering me for a while. I work in finance, and I go to these arts events and panels around Cincinnati sometimes — networking, mostly, though sometimes the food is actually decent, depending on who’s sponsoring it — and I’ve gotten this strong sense that certain people in those circles are keeping track of which clients I meet with.\n\nIt’s not like anyone has said it outright. It’s more… the invitations I get, the topics on panels, who ends up sitting near me at events. It feels like little signals. And I try to tell myself, okay, maybe that’s just coincidence, people sit where they sit, committees pick topics months ahead — but I can’t really shake it. It stays with me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling like you're being tracked or sent signals at these events?\n\nPatient: I’d say… about two months now. Maybe a little more, but two months is when I really started noticing it and couldn’t brush it off.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the usual networking stuff — people talk, people know people, Cincinnati can feel weirdly small that way — but then it kept happening, like the seating or the timing of invitations after certain client meetings. So, yeah… around two months that it’s been sticking with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about being tracked or sent signals every day, or are there days when you don't think about it at all?\n\nPatient: Most days, yeah, it comes up. Not like I’m sitting there every minute thinking about it, but if I get an invitation or I see a panel topic, or even I remember who was near me at one of those events, it sort of starts again.\n\nThere are maybe days where work is busy and I’m focused on client stuff, but even then it’s in the background. Like I’ll tell myself, “Okay, maybe it’s coincidence,” because I know committees plan these things and Cincinnati is small — I mean, everybody seems to know somebody through the arts boards or finance circles — but I can’t really let it go. It keeps feeling like they’re quietly tracking who I meet with and sending little signals.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe these people are actually tracking you or sending you signals, or is it just a feeling you can't shake?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know how it sounds, so part of me wants to say it’s just a feeling. But honestly, it feels more real than that. I’ve been pretty convinced that certain people in those arts and finance circles here are keeping track of which clients I’m meeting with.\n\nIt’s things like the invitations coming at odd times, the panel topics lining up too closely, or someone ending up sitting near me after I’ve had a certain meeting. And Cincinnati’s small, you know, people overlap everywhere — boards, fundraisers, client networks — so I try to tell myself maybe it’s all coincidence. But I can’t really let it go. It feels like they’re sending signals, quietly.\n\nDoctor: How convinced are you that these people are actually tracking you or sending you signals?\n\nPatient: I’d say… pretty convinced. Not like I have a spreadsheet proving it or anything, but it’s more than just a weird feeling.\n\nIt’s the same circles, you know — arts boards, finance people, those events downtown — and then after I meet with certain clients, suddenly there’s an invitation that feels timed strangely, or a panel topic that’s a little too close, or someone ends up seated near me who shouldn’t really be part of that conversation. And Cincinnati’s funny that way, everybody overlaps, like you go to one fundraiser and then suddenly someone from a totally different firm knows your whole week somehow.\n\nSo I do try to tell myself, okay, maybe committees planned this months ago, maybe it’s coincidence. But I can’t really let it go. I came in because I’ve been convinced, at least most of the time, that they’re quietly tracking who I’m meeting with and sending little signals about it.\n\nDoctor: Has this feeling of being tracked affected your relationships with friends, family, or colleagues?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. I’m still working, I mean, I still have clients and I can do the actual advising part fine when I’m sitting with them. But it’s… it’s gotten harder to keep the practice at the same size.\n\nLike, I’ll miss follow-ups because I’m second-guessing whether reaching out to someone is going to put me back into one of those circles, or I’ll avoid going to a public panel I probably should attend. And those panels are where you run into people, you know — you see the same faces from the museum board or someone’s charity thing, and then somehow that connects back to a client lunch, and it just gets messy in my head.\n\nSo with colleagues, I guess yes, because I’m not as present. I don’t keep up the same level of client contact I used to. My practice has gotten smaller, not collapsed or anything, but smaller. Friends and family… less directly, maybe, but work is the main place it shows.\n\nDoctor: Are there activities outside of work that you've stopped doing or avoided because of this feeling of being tracked?\n\nPatient: Yeah… outside of work too, I guess. Not in some dramatic way, like I’m locked in my apartment or anything. I still do things, sort of, in pieces.\n\nBut I’ve skipped jazz nights I used to go to, and pickup basketball, things like that. Sometimes it’s because I don’t want to be around the same overlapping crowd, but sometimes it’s just… I don’t have the spark for it. Like I’ll think about going, and then I just don’t, and I don’t even feel that bothered.\n\nFriends will text and ask what’s going on, and I’ll give these short answers. Not because I’m mad at them. Talking just feels like more effort than it should. Even with work, I still get things done, but follow-ups sit there longer than they should. It’s all kind of slowed down.\n\nDoctor: Are there any everyday tasks or routines that you've had difficulty with because of these feelings of being tracked?\n\nPatient: Yeah, the routine stuff is mostly work routines, I guess. Like follow-ups. I’ll mean to call someone back or send the email after a meeting, and then I start thinking about whether that contact pulls me back into one of those circles, and I put it off.\n\nIt sounds small, but that’s kind of the whole practice, you know — keeping contact, staying visible, going to panels where people remember you. I used to be better about that. Now I skip some of those public things, and then I’m not seeing clients or colleagues as much, and the client contact drops off.\n\nSo I’m still working. I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t function. But my practice has definitely gotten smaller because I’m not keeping up with those routines the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these signals or the tracking are meant to harm you or benefit you in some way?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s exactly to help me. Harm… I don’t know, maybe not physically or anything. It feels more like they’re trying to keep tabs on me, steer me a little, or see who I’m meeting with.\n\nLike someone will invite me to an arts fundraiser, and then the panel topic is weirdly close to something I talked about with a client, or someone from the finance side sits near me, and I start thinking, okay, that’s not random. And Cincinnati is small in that way — you see the same people at museum things, charity boards, client lunches — so it all overlaps.\n\nI tell myself it could just be coincidence, but I can’t really let it go. It feels like signals, like they’re watching which clients I’m connected to. Not necessarily “we’re going to hurt you,” but definitely not innocent either.\n\nDoctor: Are there any parts of your daily life, outside of work and social events, where you feel like these beliefs are affecting your behavior or decisions?\n\nPatient: Outside of work and social stuff… I mean, it mostly shows up through the work routines, even when I’m at home. Like I’ll be sitting there after dinner thinking, okay, I need to send that follow-up or confirm a lunch, and then I start turning it over too much and it doesn’t get done.\n\nIt’s funny, I used to be pretty good at keeping all those little threads going. My calendar looked ridiculous, honestly — panels, coffees, client check-ins, that kind of thing. My dad used to say half the job was just showing up where people could see you, which annoyed me, but he wasn’t wrong.\n\nNow I’m still working, I don’t want to make it sound like everything stopped. But the practice has gotten smaller. I miss follow-ups, I avoid some public panels, and I’m not keeping up the same level of client contact I used to. So I guess, yeah, it affects decisions day to day, just in these boring little ways that add up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people would agree that these signals and tracking are real?\n\nPatient: I think… most people would probably say I’m reading too much into it, yeah. Especially if they’re not in those circles. They’d say Cincinnati is just small, everyone knows everyone, panel topics repeat, seating is random — that kind of thing.\n\nBut when you’re actually there, and you see the same arts people and finance people crossing over, and then an invitation shows up right after I meet a client, or the topic sounds a little too close to something I was just dealing with… it’s hard for me to just shrug that off.\n\nSo, would other people agree? Maybe not out loud. Some might. I don’t know. I just know I came in because I’ve been pretty convinced for a while that certain people are quietly tracking who I meet with and sending these little signals through invitations, panels, even who sits near me. I can tell myself it might be coincidence, but I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that the signals or tracking you believe are happening are specifically about you, or could they be about someone else?\n\nPatient: I think it’s about me, mostly. Or at least it feels centered on me — who I’m meeting, which clients I’m connected to, where I show up.\n\nI mean, maybe other people are part of it too, because those circles overlap so much here. Cincinnati finance and arts boards, fundraisers, museum events… it’s always the same handful of people, and everybody sort of knows somebody’s business. My father used to say that was just networking, and maybe that’s true, but it can feel different when you’re the one walking into the room and suddenly the panel topic sounds like something from a client conversation.\n\nSo… I don’t think it’s just some general thing happening to everyone. I came in because I’ve been pretty convinced that certain people in those arts and finance circles are quietly tracking which clients I meet with and sending little signals to me — invitations, topics, even who sits near me. I can tell myself it could be coincidence, but I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered the possibility that your interpretations of these events might be incorrect?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. I mean, I wouldn’t be here if some part of me didn’t think, okay, maybe I’m connecting dots that aren’t actually connected.\n\nBut then it’s like… I’ll tell myself it could just be Cincinnati being Cincinnati, you know, the same arts board people and finance people showing up everywhere. My father used to joke that you can’t get coffee downtown without three people knowing about it by lunch, which is probably true. And then I’ll get an invitation, or a panel topic will line up a little too neatly with a client I just met, or someone gets seated near me at an event, and I’m right back to thinking, no, this is directed at me.\n\nSo yes, I’ve considered I might be wrong. I just can’t really let go of the feeling that certain people are quietly tracking who I meet with and sending signals through those things.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that you might be completely wrong about people tracking you and sending you signals?\n\nPatient: Completely wrong? I mean… I guess it’s possible. I can say that, sitting here. Like, on paper, sure — invitations go out to lots of people, panels get planned months ahead, seating charts are probably some event planner trying to balance donors and board members. My father used to always say Cincinnati feels like a big neighborhood pretending to be a city, so overlaps happen.\n\nBut then when I’m actually in it, it doesn’t feel random. I came in because for a while now I’ve been pretty convinced certain people in the arts and finance circles here are quietly tracking which clients I meet with and sending me little signals — through invitations, panel topics, even who ends up sitting near me at events.\n\nSo… possible I’m completely wrong, yes. But I don’t feel completely wrong. Even when I tell myself it could be coincidence, I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a moment where you were completely certain about one of these signals, but later found out it was actually just a coincidence or misunderstanding?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a clean example like that… where I was sure and then someone proved it was nothing. I don’t know that I have one that neat.\n\nThere have been times I’ve told myself afterward, “Okay, maybe that was just the seating chart,” or “maybe that panel was planned before I even met that client.” But then I start thinking about who was in the room, who sent the invitation, how Cincinnati’s arts and finance people all overlap — I mean, those museum dinners and fundraiser committees, it’s not like strangers, it’s the same names circling around — and it starts feeling intentional again.\n\nSo… I can’t say I’ve found out for sure it was just a coincidence. That’s kind of the problem. Even when I try to explain it that way, I don’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any evidence that would make you change your mind about people tracking you and sending you signals?\n\nPatient: I mean… evidence. Yeah, I’ve thought about that a little, but it gets slippery.\n\nIf someone could show me, I guess, like actual planning emails or something that proves the invitation list was set before I met a client, or that the panel topic had nothing to do with me… maybe that would help. Or if the seating was just automated or handled by someone who doesn’t know me at all.\n\nBut then I hear myself saying that and I think, well, people in Cincinnati know people. Especially in the arts and finance circles — it’s all donors, boards, clients, gallery people, the same handful of last names. My dad used to point that out constantly, like you’d go to one fundraiser and end up seeing half of downtown there.\n\nSo I guess there is evidence that might make me pause. But honestly, even when I tell myself it could all just be coincidence, I can’t really let it go. I came in because I’ve been pretty convinced people are tracking who I meet with and sending little signals through invitations, topics, who sits near me… and it still feels that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered that your beliefs about being tracked might be a misunderstanding that could change if you got more information?\n\nPatient: I’ve considered it, yes. I mean, I’m not trying to be impossible about it. There are times when I think, okay, maybe I’m stitching things together because Cincinnati is… you know, it’s small in that weird way, especially with arts boards and finance people and donors, everybody knowing everybody’s business.\n\nBut I came in because for a while now I’ve been convinced that certain people in those circles are quietly tracking which clients I meet with and then sending me signals — invitations that show up right after a meeting, panel topics that feel too close, certain people seated near me at events. And even when I tell myself, “Maybe if I had more information, maybe it’s just coincidence,” I can’t really let it go. It still feels like there’s something behind it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F22", "F23"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

I came in because I’ve been pretty convinced that certain people in those arts and finance circles are quietly tracking which clients I meet with and sending little signals to me — invitations, topics, even who sits near me. I can tell myself it could be coincidence, but I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered the possibility that your interpretations of these events might be incorrect?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. I mean, I wouldn’t be here if some part of me didn’t think, okay, maybe I’m connecting dots that aren’t actually connected.\n\nBut then it’s like… I’ll tell myself it could just be Cincinnati being Cincinnati, you know, the same arts board people and finance people showing up everywhere. My father used to joke that you can’t get coffee downtown without three people knowing about it by lunch, which is probably true. And then I’ll get an invitation, or a panel topic will line up a little too neatly with a client I just met, or someone gets seated near me at an event, and I’m right back to thinking, no, this is directed at me.\n\nSo yes, I’ve considered I might be wrong. I just can’t really let go of the feeling that certain people are quietly tracking who I meet with and sending signals through those things.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that you might be completely wrong about people tracking you and sending you signals?\n\nPatient: Completely wrong? I mean… I guess it’s possible. I can say that, sitting here. Like, on paper, sure — invitations go out to lots of people, panels get planned months ahead, seating charts are probably some event planner trying to balance donors and board members. My father used to always say Cincinnati feels like a big neighborhood pretending to be a city, so overlaps happen.\n\nBut then when I’m actually in it, it doesn’t feel random. I came in because for a while now I’ve been pretty convinced certain people in the arts and finance circles here are quietly tracking which clients I meet with and sending me little signals — through invitations, panel topics, even who ends up sitting near me at events.\n\nSo… possible I’m completely wrong, yes. But I don’t feel completely wrong. Even when I tell myself it could be coincidence, I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a moment where you were completely certain about one of these signals, but later found out it was actually just a coincidence or misunderstanding?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a clean example like that… where I was sure and then someone proved it was nothing. I don’t know that I have one that neat.\n\nThere have been times I’ve told myself afterward, “Okay, maybe that was just the seating chart,” or “maybe that panel was planned before I even met that client.” But then I start thinking about who was in the room, who sent the invitation, how Cincinnati’s arts and finance people all overlap — I mean, those museum dinners and fundraiser committees, it’s not like strangers, it’s the same names circling around — and it starts feeling intentional again.\n\nSo… I can’t say I’ve found out for sure it was just a coincidence. That’s kind of the problem. Even when I try to explain it that way, I don’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any evidence that would make you change your mind about people tracking you and sending you signals?\n\nPatient: I mean… evidence. Yeah, I’ve thought about that a little, but it gets slippery.\n\nIf someone could show me, I guess, like actual planning emails or something that proves the invitation list was set before I met a client, or that the panel topic had nothing to do with me… maybe that would help. Or if the seating was just automated or handled by someone who doesn’t know me at all.\n\nBut then I hear myself saying that and I think, well, people in Cincinnati know people. Especially in the arts and finance circles — it’s all donors, boards, clients, gallery people, the same handful of last names. My dad used to point that out constantly, like you’d go to one fundraiser and end up seeing half of downtown there.\n\nSo I guess there is evidence that might make me pause. But honestly, even when I tell myself it could all just be coincidence, I can’t really let it go. I came in because I’ve been pretty convinced people are tracking who I meet with and sending little signals through invitations, topics, who sits near me… and it still feels that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered that your beliefs about being tracked might be a misunderstanding that could change if you got more information?\n\nPatient: I’ve considered it, yes. I mean, I’m not trying to be impossible about it. There are times when I think, okay, maybe I’m stitching things together because Cincinnati is… you know, it’s small in that weird way, especially with arts boards and finance people and donors, everybody knowing everybody’s business.\n\nBut I came in because for a while now I’ve been convinced that certain people in those circles are quietly tracking which clients I meet with and then sending me signals — invitations that show up right after a meeting, panel topics that feel too close, certain people seated near me at events. And even when I tell myself, “Maybe if I had more information, maybe it’s just coincidence,” I can’t really let it go. It still feels like there’s something behind it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah, my sister has said that to me pretty directly. Not in a mean way, more like, “I think you’re reading into this too much,” or “I don’t think anyone is tracking your client meetings.” And I’ll say, okay, maybe, I hear you.

But then it’s hard because she doesn’t really know those circles here. Like, Cincinnati can look bigger from the outside, but if you’re around the arts events or the finance fundraisers, it’s the same people on boards, the same donors, the same firms sponsoring things. I remember my dad always talking about who was connected to who at those dinners, which is probably partly why I notice it now.

So when someone tells me it’s not true, I try to act reasonable. I’ll even tell myself, “Fine, maybe the invitation timing was random, maybe the panel topic was planned months ago, maybe the seating was just assigned.” But I don’t really feel convinced. I still come back to this feeling that people are paying attention to who I meet with and then sending these little signals through the events and invitations. I can’t quite shake it.

It’s more… the invitations I get, the topics on panels, who ends up sitting near me at events. It feels like little signals. Maybe a little more, but two months is when I really started noticing it and couldn’t brush it off.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the usual networking stuff — people talk, people know people, Cincinnati can feel weirdly small that way — but then it kept happening, like the seating or the timing of invitations after certain client meetings.
